Q: Fred grew 38 cantelopes . Tim grew 44 cantelopes . How many cantelopes did they grow in total ? 
A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- Fred grew 38 cantaloupes.  
- Tim grew 44 cantaloupes.  
- We are tasked with finding the total number of cantaloupes grown by Fred and Tim combined.  
- Assume no cantaloupes were lost, shared, or discarded, and the numbers provided are accurate.  

---

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
**Method 1:** Direct addition of the two numbers: Add Fred's cantaloupes (38) to Tim's cantaloupes (44).  
**Method 2:** Break the numbers into parts for easier mental math: Decompose 38 and 44 into tens and ones, then add the parts step by step.  

---

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
1. Add Fred's cantaloupes (38) to Tim's cantaloupes (44):  
   \( 38 + 44 \).  
2. Perform the addition:  
   - Add the ones digits: \( 8 + 4 = 12 \).  
   - Write down the 2 and carry over the 1 to the tens place.  
   - Add the tens digits: \( 3 + 4 = 7 \), then add the carried-over 1: \( 7 + 1 = 8 \).  
   - Combine the results: \( 38 + 44 = 82 \).  
3. Double-check the addition:  
   - Recalculate step by step: \( 38 + 44 = 82 \).  

**Potential pitfalls:**  
- Misplacing the carry-over digit could lead to an incorrect result.  

---

**Method 2:**  
1. Decompose the numbers into tens and ones:  
   - \( 38 = 30 + 8 \).  
   - \( 44 = 40 + 4 \).  
2. Add the tens parts first:  
   \( 30 + 40 = 70 \).  
3. Add the ones parts next:  
   \( 8 + 4 = 12 \).  
4. Combine the results:  
   \( 70 + 12 = 82 \).  
5. Double-check the addition:  
   - Recalculate step by step: \( 30 + 40 = 70 \), \( 8 + 4 = 12 \), \( 70 + 12 = 82 \).  

**Potential pitfalls:**  
- Forgetting to combine the tens and ones parts at the end could lead to an incomplete result.  

---

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves fewer steps and is straightforward for simple addition problems.  
- **Method 2** is useful for mental math or breaking down larger numbers, but it introduces more intermediate steps, which could increase the chance of error.  
- For this problem, **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and reliability.  

---

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- Using **Method 1**:  
  \( 38 + 44 = 82 \).  

---

Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.  
- Recalculate using **Method 2**:  
  \( 30 + 40 = 70 \), \( 8 + 4 = 12 \), \( 70 + 12 = 82 \).  
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution is correct.  

---

The answer is **82**.

A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Kendall drove **0.16666666666666666 miles** with her mother.
- Kendall drove **0.5 miles** with her father.
- We are tasked with finding the total distance Kendall drove.
- Assumptions:
  - The distances are measured accurately.
  - There are no additional distances driven beyond those stated.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct addition of the two distances.**
- Add the two distances as decimals: \( 0.16666666666666666 + 0.5 \).
- Perform the addition step by step, ensuring precision.

**Method 2: Convert the repeating decimal to a fraction, then add.**
- Convert \( 0.16666666666666666 \) (a repeating decimal) to its fractional equivalent \( \frac{1}{6} \).
- Convert \( 0.5 \) to its fractional equivalent \( \frac{1}{2} \).
- Add the fractions using a common denominator, then convert the result back to a decimal.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition of decimals**
1. Write the two distances: \( 0.16666666666666666 \) and \( 0.5 \).
2. Align the decimals and add:
   \[
   0.16666666666666666 + 0.5 = 0.66666666666666666
   \]
3. The result is \( 0.66666666666666666 \), which is a repeating decimal.

**Potential pitfalls:**
- Ensure precision when working with repeating decimals.
- Avoid rounding prematurely to maintain accuracy.

---

#### **Method 2: Convert to fractions and add**
1. Convert \( 0.16666666666666666 \) to a fraction:
   - Recognize that \( 0.16666666666666666 \) is a repeating decimal equivalent to \( \frac{1}{6} \).
2. Convert \( 0.5 \) to a fraction:
   - \( 0.5 = \frac{1}{2} \).
3. Add the fractions:
   - Find a common denominator for \( \frac{1}{6} \) and \( \frac{1}{2} \). The least common denominator (LCD) is 6.
   - Rewrite the fractions with the LCD:
     \[
     \frac{1}{6} + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{1}{6} + \frac{3}{6} = \frac{4}{6}.
     \]
   - Simplify \( \frac{4}{6} \) to \( \frac{2}{3} \).
4. Convert \( \frac{2}{3} \) back to a decimal:
   - \( \frac{2}{3} = 0.66666666666666666 \).

**Potential pitfalls:**
- Ensure proper fraction simplification.
- Avoid errors when converting between fractions and decimals.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Direct addition of decimals):**
  - Advantage: Straightforward and quick.
  - Disadvantage: Requires careful handling of repeating decimals to avoid rounding errors.
- **Method 2 (Convert to fractions):**
  - Advantage: Provides exact results and avoids issues with repeating decimals.
  - Disadvantage: Slightly more complex due to fraction operations and conversions.

**Conclusion:** Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 2** is more precise and avoids potential pitfalls with repeating decimals. Therefore, Method 2 is preferred.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

1. Convert \( 0.16666666666666666 \) to \( \frac{1}{6} \) and \( 0.5 \) to \( \frac{1}{2} \).
2. Add the fractions:
   \[
   \frac{1}{6} + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{1}{6} + \frac{3}{6} = \frac{4}{6}.
   \]
3. Simplify \( \frac{4}{6} \) to \( \frac{2}{3} \).

Q: Jason has 676 Pokemon cards . Alyssa bought 224 of Jason 's Pokemon cards . How many Pokemon cards does Jason have now ? 
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Jason originally has **676 Pokemon cards**.
- Alyssa buys **224 of Jason's Pokemon cards**.
- We are tasked with finding how many Pokemon cards Jason has left.
- Assumptions:
  - All 224 cards are subtracted from Jason's collection.
  - No additional cards are gained or lost during the transaction.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Subtraction  
- Subtract the number of cards Alyssa bought (224) from Jason's original total (676). This is a straightforward subtraction problem.

**Method 2:** Complementary addition  
- Start with the number of cards Alyssa bought (224) and add to it the number of cards Jason has left to reach the original total (676). Solve for the missing number using the equation: \( 224 + x = 676 \).

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction**
1. Start with Jason's original total: **676**.
2. Subtract the number of cards Alyssa bought:  
   \[
   676 - 224
   \]
3. Perform the subtraction step by step:  
   - Subtract the ones place: \( 6 - 4 = 2 \).  
   - Subtract the tens place: \( 7 - 2 = 5 \).  
   - Subtract the hundreds place: \( 6 - 2 = 4 \).  
   - Result: \( 676 - 224 = 452 \).

4. Double-check the subtraction by adding the result (452) to the number of cards Alyssa bought (224):  
   \[
   452 + 224 = 676
   \]  
   The calculation checks out.

#### **Method 2: Complementary addition**
1. Set up the equation:  
   \[
   224 + x = 676
   \]
2. Solve for \( x \) by subtracting 224 from 676:  
   \[
   x = 676 - 224
   \]
3. Perform the subtraction step by step (as in Method 1):  
   - Subtract the ones place: \( 6 - 4 = 2 \).  
   - Subtract the tens place: \( 7 - 2 = 5 \).  
   - Subtract the hundreds place: \( 6 - 2 = 4 \).  
   - Result: \( x = 452 \).

4. Double-check by substituting \( x = 452 \) back into the original equation:  
   \[
   224 + 452 = 676
   \]  
   The calculation checks out.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Subtraction):**  
  - Direct and efficient.  
  - Involves fewer steps and is less prone to error.  
  - Best suited for straightforward subtraction problems like this one.

- **Method 2 (Complementary addition):**  
  - Useful for verifying results or solving problems where the missing value is part of a sum.  
  - Slightly more complex due to the need to rearrange the equation.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is the better choice for this problem because it is simpler and more direct.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1 (Subtraction):**  
\[
676 - 224 = 452
\]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Add the result (452) to the number of cards Alyssa bought (224):  
  \[
  452 + 224 = 676
  \]  
  The calculation checks out, confirming the solution is correct.

The answer is **452**.

A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- Mary is baking a cake and the recipe requires 8 cups of flour.  
- She has already added 2 cups of flour.  
- We need to determine how many more cups of flour she needs to add to reach the total of 8 cups.  
- Assume no flour is spilled or wasted, and the recipe's requirement is exact.

---

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
**Method 1:** Subtract the amount of flour already added (2 cups) from the total required (8 cups). This directly calculates the remaining amount.  
**Method 2:** Use addition to find the missing amount. Start with 2 cups and add incrementally until the total equals 8 cups. This method is more iterative but can confirm the result.

---

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
- Start with the total required amount: 8 cups.  
- Subtract the amount already added: 8 - 2 = 6.  
- Double-check: If Mary adds 6 more cups to the 2 cups already added, the total will be 2 + 6 = 8 cups, which matches the recipe's requirement.  
- Potential pitfalls: This method is straightforward, but errors could occur if subtraction is performed incorrectly or if the initial values are misinterpreted.

**Method 2:**  
- Start with the amount already added: 2 cups.  
- Incrementally add flour until the total equals 8 cups:  
  - 2 + 1 = 3 (still not enough).  
  - 3 + 1 = 4 (still not enough).  
  - 4 + 1 = 5 (still not enough).  
  - 5 + 1 = 6 (still not enough).  
  - 6 + 1 = 7 (still not enough).  
  - 7 + 1 = 8 (now matches the recipe).  
- Total added: 6 cups.  
- Double-check: Adding 6 cups to the initial 2 cups results in 2 + 6 = 8 cups, which matches the recipe's requirement.  
- Potential pitfalls: This method is more time-consuming and prone to errors if the iterative process is not carefully tracked.

---

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- **Method 1** is more efficient and direct, as it uses subtraction to immediately calculate the missing amount. It is less prone to errors and requires fewer steps.  
- **Method 2** is more iterative and time-consuming, but it can be useful for confirming the result through a step-by-step approach. However, it is less practical for larger numbers.  

---

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and reliability.  
- Solution: Subtract the amount already added from the total required: 8 - 2 = 6.  

---

Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.  
- Verify: If Mary adds 6 more cups to the 2 cups already added, the total will be 2 + 6 = 8 cups, which matches the recipe's requirement.  
- The calculation checks out, and the result is reasonable.

Q: Joan picked 43 apples from the orchard , and gave 27 apples to Melanie . How many apples does Joan have now ? 
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Joan picked 43 apples from the orchard.
- Joan gave 27 apples to Melanie.
- We are tasked with finding how many apples Joan has left.
- Assumptions:
  - No apples were lost, damaged, or otherwise unaccounted for.
  - The subtraction operation is valid and represents the apples Joan gave away.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Direct subtraction. Subtract the number of apples Joan gave to Melanie (27) from the total number of apples Joan originally had (43). This method is straightforward and efficient.

**Method 2:** Use addition to verify subtraction. Start with the number of apples Joan has left (unknown) and add the number of apples she gave away (27). The result should equal the original total (43). This method checks the subtraction indirectly.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Start with the total number of apples Joan picked: 43.
2. Subtract the number of apples Joan gave to Melanie:  
   \[
   43 - 27 = 16
   \]
3. Double-check the subtraction:
   - Break it into smaller steps for clarity:
     - Subtract the ones place: \(3 - 7\). Since 3 is smaller than 7, borrow 1 from the tens place:
       - \(13 - 7 = 6\).
     - Subtract the tens place: \(4 - 2 = 2\).
     - Combine results: \(43 - 27 = 16\).
4. Verify the result by reverse addition:
   - Add the apples Joan has left (16) to the apples she gave away (27):  
     \[
     16 + 27 = 43
     \]
   - The result matches the original total, confirming the subtraction.

#### **Method 2: Use addition to verify subtraction**
1. Assume Joan has \(x\) apples left after giving 27 to Melanie.
2. Add the apples Joan gave away (27) to the apples she has left (\(x\)):  
   \[
   x + 27 = 43
   \]
3. Solve for \(x\) by subtracting 27 from both sides:  
   \[
   x = 43 - 27 = 16
   \]
4. Double-check the addition:
   - Add \(x = 16\) to 27:  
     \[
     16 + 27 = 43
     \]
   - The result matches the original total, confirming the calculation.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves a single subtraction operation and a straightforward verification step. It is less prone to errors and requires fewer steps.
- **Method 2** is useful for verifying the subtraction indirectly but introduces an extra step (solving for \(x\)) that may not be necessary for simple problems like this one.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and efficiency.
- Solution:
  \[
  43 - 27 = 16
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Reverse addition to verify:
  \[
  16 + 27 = 43
  \]
- The result matches the original total, confirming the solution is correct.

The answer is **16**.

  - No employees are unaccounted for in the given numbers.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct addition**  
- Add the number of part-time employees to the number of full-time employees: \( 2041 + 63093 \).  
- This method is straightforward and involves a single addition operation.

**Method 2: Break into smaller steps**  
- Break the addition into smaller, more manageable parts:
  - Add the thousands place first: \( 2000 + 63000 \).
  - Then add the remaining smaller numbers: \( 41 + 93 \).
  - Finally, combine the results of the two smaller sums.
- This method is useful for mental math or verifying the result.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition**
1. Write the numbers vertically for clarity:
   ```
     63093
   +   2041
   --------
   ```
2. Add column by column:
   - Units place: \( 3 + 1 = 4 \).
   - Tens place: \( 9 + 4 = 13 \) (write 3, carry 1).
   - Hundreds place: \( 0 + 0 + 1 = 1 \).
   - Thousands place: \( 3 + 2 = 5 \).
   - Ten-thousands place: \( 6 + 0 = 6 \).
3. Combine the results: \( 63093 + 2041 = 65134 \).

#### **Method 2: Break into smaller steps**
1. Add the thousands place first:
   - \( 2000 + 63000 = 65000 \).
2. Add the remaining smaller numbers:
   - \( 41 + 93 = 134 \).
3. Combine the two results:
   - \( 65000 + 134 = 65134 \).

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves a single addition operation. It is ideal when using a calculator or writing the numbers out.
- **Method 2** is more intuitive for mental math or breaking the problem into smaller, simpler steps. However, it introduces more intermediate steps, which could increase the chance of error.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is better for clarity and reliability in this case.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Q: Tim 's cat had kittens . He gave 3 to Jessica and 6 to Sara . He now has 9 kittens . How many kittens did he have to start with ? 
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Tim gave away 3 kittens to Jessica and 6 kittens to Sara.
- After giving away these kittens, Tim still has 9 kittens left.
- We are tasked with finding how many kittens Tim had to start with.
- Assumptions:
  - No kittens were lost, born, or otherwise unaccounted for during this process.
  - The total number of kittens Tim started with equals the kittens he gave away plus the kittens he still has.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Addition of kittens given away and kittens remaining.**
- Add the number of kittens Tim gave away (3 to Jessica and 6 to Sara) to the number of kittens he still has (9). This will give the total number of kittens Tim started with.

**Method 2: Reverse subtraction.**
- Start with the number of kittens Tim has now (9) and add back the kittens he gave away (3 to Jessica and 6 to Sara). This will reconstruct the original total.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Addition of kittens given away and kittens remaining**
1. Calculate the total number of kittens Tim gave away:
   - 3 (to Jessica) + 6 (to Sara) = 9 kittens given away.
2. Add the kittens given away to the kittens Tim still has:
   - 9 (kittens given away) + 9 (kittens remaining) = 18 kittens.
3. Double-check:
   - If Tim started with 18 kittens, gave away 9, and kept 9, the numbers match.

**Potential pitfalls:**
- Forgetting to add both groups of kittens given away (Jessica's and Sara's) could lead to an incorrect total.

---

#### **Method 2: Reverse subtraction**
1. Start with the number of kittens Tim has now:
   - 9 kittens.
2. Add back the kittens Tim gave away:
   - Add 3 (to Jessica): 9 + 3 = 12.
   - Add 6 (to Sara): 12 + 6 = 18.
3. Double-check:
   - If Tim started with 18 kittens, gave away 9 (3 + 6), and kept 9, the numbers match.

**Potential pitfalls:**
- Forgetting to add both groups of kittens given away (Jessica's and Sara's) could lead to an incorrect total.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Clarity:** Both methods are straightforward, but Method 1 (addition) is slightly more intuitive because it directly combines the kittens given away and the kittens remaining.
- **Reliability:** Both methods are equally reliable if executed correctly.
- **Efficiency:** Method 1 is slightly more efficient because it combines all the kittens given away in one step before adding to the kittens remaining.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

**Method 1 is chosen for its clarity and efficiency.**

1. Calculate the total number of kittens Tim gave away:
   - 3 (to Jessica) + 6 (to Sara) = 9 kittens given away.
2. Add the kittens given away to the kittens Tim still has:
   - 9 (kittens given away) + 9 (kittens remaining) = 18 kittens.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- If Tim started with 18 kittens, gave away 9 (3 to Jessica and 6 to Sara), and kept 9, the numbers balance:
  - 18 (original total) - 9 (given away) = 9 (remaining).
- The solution is consistent and reasonable.

---

**The answer is 18.**

Q: A company painted some houses in Hancock County white and blue using a total of 6689 gallons of paint . If they used 660 gallons of white paint , how many gallons of blue paint did the company use ? 
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The company used a total of 6689 gallons of paint to paint houses in Hancock County.
- Of this total, 660 gallons were white paint.
- The remaining paint was blue paint.
- We assume no paint was wasted or used for other purposes, and the total paint used is the sum of white and blue paint.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Subtract the amount of white paint from the total paint to find the amount of blue paint:  
\[ \text{Blue paint} = \text{Total paint} - \text{White paint} \]

**Method 2:** Use a complementary approach:  
If the total paint is divided into white and blue, the fraction of blue paint can be calculated as:  
\[ \text{Blue paint} = \text{Total paint} - \text{White paint} \]  
This method is essentially the same as Method 1 but emphasizes the complementary relationship between white and blue paint.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Start with the total amount of paint: 6689 gallons.
2. Subtract the amount of white paint:  
   \[ 6689 - 660 = 6029 \]
3. Verify the calculation:  
   - Break it into smaller steps:  
     \[ 6689 - 600 = 6089 \]  
     \[ 6089 - 60 = 6029 \]  
   - Double-check using addition:  
     \[ 6029 + 660 = 6689 \]  
   The calculation checks out.

#### **Method 2: Complementary approach**
1. Recognize that the total paint is divided into white and blue paint.  
   \[ \text{Blue paint} = \text{Total paint} - \text{White paint} \]
2. Perform the subtraction:  
   \[ 6689 - 660 = 6029 \]
3. Verify the calculation:  
   - Break it into smaller steps:  
     \[ 6689 - 600 = 6089 \]  
     \[ 6089 - 60 = 6029 \]  
   - Double-check using addition:  
     \[ 6029 + 660 = 6689 \]  
   The calculation checks out.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and efficient, directly solving the problem with minimal steps. It is easy to understand and avoids unnecessary complexity.
- **Method 2** emphasizes the complementary relationship between white and blue paint but ultimately performs the same subtraction. It does not offer any additional clarity or efficiency compared to Method 1.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and directness.
- Solution:  
  \[ 6689 - 660 = 6029 \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify by adding the white and blue paint amounts:  
  \[ 6029 + 660 = 6689 \]  
  The total matches the given information, confirming the solution is correct.

### Final Answer:
The company used **6029 gallons of blue paint**.  
The answer is **6029**.

A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The park currently has **47 orchid bushes**.
- Park workers will plant **37 orchid bushes today**.
- Park workers will plant **25 orchid bushes tomorrow**.
- The total number of workers involved is **35**, but this number is irrelevant to the calculation of the total orchid bushes.
- We assume no orchid bushes are removed, damaged, or lost during the planting process.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Add the current number of orchid bushes to the number planted today and tomorrow:  
\[ \text{Total bushes} = 47 + 37 + 25 \]

**Method 2:**  
Combine the bushes planted today and tomorrow first, then add the result to the current number of bushes:  
\[ \text{Total bushes} = 47 + (37 + 25) \]

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition of all numbers**
1. Start with the current number of bushes: **47**.
2. Add the bushes planted today:  
   \[ 47 + 37 = 84 \]
3. Add the bushes planted tomorrow:  
   \[ 84 + 25 = 109 \]
4. Final result: **109 orchid bushes**.

- This method is straightforward and involves simple addition. There is minimal risk of error if calculations are done carefully.

#### **Method 2: Grouping planted bushes first**
1. Combine the bushes planted today and tomorrow:  
   \[ 37 + 25 = 62 \]
2. Add the result to the current number of bushes:  
   \[ 47 + 62 = 109 \]
3. Final result: **109 orchid bushes**.

**Check for pitfalls:**  
- This method involves grouping numbers first, which can simplify mental math. However, errors could occur if the intermediate sum (37 + 25) is calculated incorrectly.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and intuitive, as it simply adds all the numbers in sequence. It is less prone to errors because it avoids intermediate grouping.
- **Method 2** is slightly more efficient for mental math, as grouping smaller numbers (37 + 25) first can simplify the addition process. However, it introduces an extra intermediate step, which could lead to errors if not done carefully.

**Conclusion:** Both methods are valid and yield the same result, but **Method 1** is slightly clearer and more reliable for this problem.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:

2. Add the bushes planted today:  
   \[ 47 + 37 = 84 \]
3. Add the bushes planted tomorrow:  
   \[ 84 + 25 = 109 \]

   \[ 37 + 25 = 62 \]  
   \[ 47 + 62 = 109 \]

A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Tom bought three items:
  1. A skateboard for $9.46.
  2. Marbles for $9.56.
  3. Shorts for $14.50.
- The problem asks for the total amount Tom spent on **toys**.
- Assumption: Only the skateboard and marbles are considered "toys," while the shorts are not.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Add the prices of the skateboard and marbles directly: $9.46 + $9.56. Ignore the price of the shorts since it is not a toy.

**Method 2:**  
Add all three items together first: $9.46 + $9.56 + $14.50. Then subtract the price of the shorts ($14.50) to isolate the total spent on toys.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition of toy prices**
1. Add the price of the skateboard and marbles:
   \[
   9.46 + 9.56
   \]
2. Align the decimal points and add:
   \[
   9.46  
+  9.56  
\------
   19.02
   \]
3. Result: The total spent on toys is **$19.02**.

#### **Method 2: Add all items, then subtract the shorts**
1. Add the prices of all three items:
   \[
   9.46 + 9.56 + 14.50
   \]
2. Align the decimal points and add step by step:
   - First, add $9.46 and $9.56:
     \[
     9.46  
  +  9.56  
  \------
     19.02
     \]
   - Then, add $19.02 and $14.50:
     \[
     19.02  
  +  14.50  
  \------
     33.52
     \]
3. Subtract the price of the shorts ($14.50) from the total:
   \[
   33.52 - 14.50
   \]
4. Align the decimal points and subtract:
   \[
   33.52  
-  14.50  
\------
   19.02
   \]
5. Result: The total spent on toys is **$19.02**.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it focuses only on the relevant items (toys) and avoids unnecessary calculations.
- **Method 2** involves extra steps (adding all items and then subtracting the shorts), which increases the chance of error and is less efficient.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is clearer and more reliable for this problem.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice.
- Add the prices of the skateboard and marbles:
  \[
  9.46 + 9.56 = 19.02
  \]
- The total spent on toys is **$19.02**.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 1:
  \[
  9.46 + 9.56 = 19.02
  \]
- Recalculate using Method 2:
  \[
  (9.46 + 9.56 + 14.50) - 14.50 = 19.02
  \]
- Both methods confirm the result. The calculations are accurate, and the result is reasonable.

Q: Sandy grew 51 pumpkins . Mike grew 23 pumpkins . How many pumpkins did they grow in total ? 
A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- Sandy grew 51 pumpkins.  
- Mike grew 23 pumpkins.  
- We are tasked with finding the total number of pumpkins they grew together.  
- Assume no pumpkins were lost, damaged, or shared between them.  

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
Method 1: Direct addition of the two quantities: Add Sandy's pumpkins (51) to Mike's pumpkins (23).  
Method 2: Break the numbers into parts for easier mental math: Decompose 51 into 50 + 1 and 23 into 20 + 3, then add the parts step by step.  

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1: Direct addition**  
- Add the two numbers directly:  
  51 + 23 = ?  
  Step 1: Add the ones place: 1 + 3 = 4.  
  Step 2: Add the tens place: 5 + 2 = 7.  
  Step 3: Combine the results: 74.  
- Double-check the addition:  
  51 + 23 = 74.  

**Method 2: Decomposition and addition**  
- Break the numbers into parts:  
  51 = 50 + 1, and 23 = 20 + 3.  
- Add the tens parts first:  
  50 + 20 = 70.  
- Add the ones parts next:  
  1 + 3 = 4.  
- Combine the results:  
  70 + 4 = 74.  
- Double-check the addition:  
  50 + 20 = 70, and 1 + 3 = 4, so 70 + 4 = 74.

Q: Dan had 14 peaches and 10 pears at his roadside fruit dish . He went to the orchard and picked peaches to stock up . There are now 85 peaches . how many did he pick ? 
A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- Dan initially had 14 peaches.  
- He picked additional peaches from the orchard.  
- After picking, there are now 85 peaches.  
- We assume no peaches were lost, eaten, or removed during this process.  

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
Method 1: Subtract the initial number of peaches from the final total to determine how many peaches Dan picked: \( 85 - 14 \).  
Method 2: Use a reverse equation: Start with the final total (85 peaches) and subtract the initial number (14 peaches) to find the difference. This is essentially the same as Method 1 but framed differently for clarity.  

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
- Start with the total number of peaches after picking: \( 85 \).  
- Subtract the initial number of peaches: \( 85 - 14 \).  
- Perform the subtraction:  
  - Break it into steps: \( 85 - 14 = (85 - 10) - 4 = 75 - 4 = 71 \).  
- Result: Dan picked \( 71 \) peaches.  

**Method 2:**  
- Frame the problem as a reverse equation: \( x + 14 = 85 \), where \( x \) is the number of peaches Dan picked.  
- Solve for \( x \):  
  - Subtract \( 14 \) from both sides: \( x = 85 - 14 \).  
  - Perform the subtraction: \( 85 - 14 = 71 \).  
- Result: Dan picked \( 71 \) peaches.  

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- Both methods are mathematically identical and yield the same result.  
- Method 1 is slightly more direct and intuitive for subtraction problems.  
- Method 2 is useful for framing the problem as an equation, which can be helpful in more complex scenarios.  

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- Method 1 is chosen for its simplicity and efficiency.  
- Solution: \( 85 - 14 = 71 \).  

Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.  
- Re-add the initial peaches and the picked peaches to confirm the total: \( 14 + 71 = 85 \).  
- The calculation checks out, and the result is reasonable.  

The answer is **71**.

  - Only turnips are relevant to the calculation; pumpkins are not included.
  - No turnips were lost, shared, or otherwise unaccounted for.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

\[ \text{Total turnips} = \text{Sally's turnips} + \text{Mary's turnips} \]

**Method 2:**  
Break the problem into smaller steps for clarity:  
1. Start with Sally's turnips (113).  
2. Add Mary's turnips (129) to Sally's total.  
3. Verify the result by re-adding the numbers in reverse order.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct Addition**
1. Add Sally's turnips (113) to Mary's turnips (129):  
   \[
   113 + 129
   \]
2. Perform the addition step by step:  
   - Add the ones place: \( 3 + 9 = 12 \) (write down 2, carry over 1).  
   - Add the tens place: \( 1 + 2 = 3 \), plus the carried-over 1 gives \( 4 \).  
   - Add the hundreds place: \( 1 + 1 = 2 \).  
   - Final result: \( 242 \).

3. Check for errors: Re-add the numbers in reverse order:  
   \[
   129 + 113 = 242
   \]  
   The result is consistent.

#### **Method 2: Step-by-Step Addition**
1. Start with Sally's turnips: \( 113 \).  
2. Add Mary's turnips: \( 129 \).  
   - Add the ones place: \( 3 + 9 = 12 \) (write down 2, carry over 1).  
   - Add the tens place: \( 1 + 2 = 3 \), plus the carried-over 1 gives \( 4 \).  
   - Add the hundreds place: \( 1 + 1 = 2 \).  
   - Final result: \( 242 \).

3. Verify by breaking the numbers into parts:  
   - Add the hundreds: \( 100 + 100 = 200 \).  
   - Add the tens: \( 10 + 20 = 30 \).  
   - Add the ones: \( 3 + 9 = 12 \).  
   - Combine: \( 200 + 30 + 12 = 242 \).  

4. Check for errors: Re-add the numbers in reverse order:  
   \[
   129 + 113 = 242
   \]  
   The result is consistent.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves a single addition step.  
- **Method 2** is more detailed and breaks the problem into smaller parts, which can help avoid errors but is more time-consuming.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is preferable for its simplicity and clarity.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

\[
113 + 129 = 242
\]

Q: Joan bought toy cars for $ 14.88 , a skateboard for $ 4.88 , and got toy trucks for $ 5.86 . She spent $ 14.55 on pants . In total , how much did Joan spend on toys ? 
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Joan bought the following items:
  - Toy cars for $14.88.
  - A skateboard for $4.88.
  - Toy trucks for $5.86.
  - Pants for $14.55.
- The question asks for the total amount Joan spent **on toys only**.
- Assumptions:
  - "Toys" include toy cars, the skateboard, and toy trucks.
  - Pants are not considered a toy and should not be included in the total.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Add the prices of the toy-related items directly: $14.88 (toy cars) + $4.88 (skateboard) + $5.86 (toy trucks). Ignore the cost of the pants.

**Method 2:**  
First, calculate the total cost of all items Joan purchased: $14.88 + $4.88 + $5.86 + $14.55. Then subtract the cost of the pants ($14.55) to isolate the total spent on toys.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition of toy-related items**
1. Add the cost of toy cars and the skateboard:
   \[
   14.88 + 4.88 = 19.76
   \]
2. Add the cost of toy trucks to the result:
   \[
   19.76 + 5.86 = 25.62
   \]
3. Double-check the calculations:
   - Adding $14.88 and $4.88:
     - $14.88 + $4.88 = $19.76 (correct).
   - Adding $19.76 and $5.86:
     - $19.76 + $5.86 = $25.62 (correct).

#### **Method 2: Subtract the cost of pants from the total**
1. Calculate the total cost of all items:
   \[
   14.88 + 4.88 + 5.86 + 14.55 = 40.17
   \]
2. Subtract the cost of the pants:
   \[
   40.17 - 14.55 = 25.62
   \]
3. Double-check the calculations:
   - Adding all items:
     - $14.88 + $4.88 = $19.76.
     - $19.76 + $5.86 = $25.62.
     - $25.62 + $14.55 = $40.17 (correct).
   - Subtracting the pants:
     - $40.17 - $14.55 = $25.62 (correct).

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it focuses only on the toy-related items, avoiding unnecessary calculations.
- **Method 2** involves more steps and introduces the possibility of errors when summing all items and then subtracting the pants. While it still works, it is less efficient for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better method.
- Add the toy-related items:
  \[
  14.88 + 4.88 = 19.76
  \]
  \[
  19.76 + 5.86 = 25.62
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 2:
  - Total cost of all items: $14.88 + $4.88 + $5.86 + $14.55 = $40.17.
  - Subtract the pants: $40.17 - $14.55 = $25.62.
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution is correct.

Q: At a pie-eating contest , Erik got through 0.6666666666666666 pie before time was called ; Frank finished just 0.3333333333333333 pie . How much more pie did Erik eat than Frank ? 
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Erik ate **0.6666666666666666** of a pie.
- Frank ate **0.3333333333333333** of a pie.
- We are tasked with finding how much **more pie Erik ate than Frank**.
- Assumptions:
  - The values are exact and represent fractions of a single pie.
  - No rounding is applied unless explicitly stated.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct subtraction**  
- Subtract the amount of pie Frank ate from the amount Erik ate: \( 0.6666666666666666 - 0.3333333333333333 \).  
- This method directly calculates the difference.

**Method 2: Convert to fractions and subtract**  
- Convert the decimal values to fractions: \( 0.6666666666666666 = \frac{2}{3} \) and \( 0.3333333333333333 = \frac{1}{3} \).  
- Subtract the fractions: \( \frac{2}{3} - \frac{1}{3} \).  
- This method avoids working with repeating decimals and uses exact arithmetic.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Write the subtraction:  
   \( 0.6666666666666666 - 0.3333333333333333 \).
2. Perform the subtraction:  
   \( 0.6666666666666666 - 0.3333333333333333 = 0.3333333333333333 \).
3. Verify the result:  
   Add the result back to Frank's portion to ensure it equals Erik's portion:  
   \( 0.3333333333333333 + 0.3333333333333333 = 0.6666666666666666 \).  
   The calculation checks out.

**Potential pitfalls:**  
- Working with repeating decimals can introduce rounding errors if not handled carefully. However, in this case, the subtraction is straightforward.

---

#### **Method 2: Convert to fractions and subtract**
1. Convert the decimals to fractions:  
   \( 0.6666666666666666 = \frac{2}{3} \) and \( 0.3333333333333333 = \frac{1}{3} \).
2. Subtract the fractions:  
   \( \frac{2}{3} - \frac{1}{3} = \frac{2 - 1}{3} = \frac{1}{3} \).
3. Verify the result:  
   Add the result back to Frank's portion to ensure it equals Erik's portion:  
   \( \frac{1}{3} + \frac{1}{3} = \frac{2}{3} \).  
   The calculation checks out.

**Potential pitfalls:**  
- Converting decimals to fractions requires recognizing repeating patterns, but this is straightforward for these values.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Direct subtraction):**  
  - Advantage: Simple and quick for small numbers.  
  - Disadvantage: Repeating decimals can lead to rounding errors in more complex problems.
- **Method 2 (Fractions):**  
  - Advantage: Exact arithmetic avoids rounding errors.  
  - Disadvantage: Slightly more effort to convert decimals to fractions.

For this problem, **both methods are equally valid** because the numbers are simple and the repeating decimals are manageable. However, **Method 2** is more precise and avoids potential pitfalls with rounding.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
We choose **Method 2 (Fractions)** for its precision.

1. Convert the decimals to fractions:  
   \( 0.6666666666666666 = \frac{2}{3} \), \( 0.3333333333333333 = \frac{1}{3} \).
2. Subtract the fractions:  
   \( \frac{2}{3} - \frac{1}{3} = \frac{2 - 1}{3} = \frac{1}{3} \).
3. Verify the result:  
   Add the result back to Frank's portion:  
   \( \frac{1}{3} + \frac{1}{3} = \frac{2}{3} \).  
   The calculation checks out.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

  \( 0.6666666666666666 - 0.3333333333333333 = 0.3333333333333333 \).

  - After removing the second seashell, Tom has 3 left.

- We are tasked with finding the total number of football games Joan attended in both years combined.

  - The number of games attended in each year is accurate.
  - There is no overlap or double-counting of games between the two years.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Direct addition of the number of games attended this year and last year: \( 4 + 9 \).  
**Method 2:** Break the problem into smaller steps by grouping numbers: Add \( 4 + 10 \), then subtract \( 1 \) to adjust for the difference between \( 9 \) and \( 10 \).

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition**
1. Add the number of games attended this year to the number attended last year:  
   \[
   4 + 9 = 13
   \]
2. Double-check the calculation:  
   - Start with \( 4 \), then count up \( 9 \): \( 4 + 1 = 5 \), \( 5 + 1 = 6 \), ..., \( 12 + 1 = 13 \).  
   - The result is consistent.

**Potential pitfalls:** None, as this is a straightforward addition problem.

---

#### **Method 2: Grouping numbers**
1. Round \( 9 \) up to \( 10 \) for easier addition:  
   \[
   4 + 10 = 14
   \]
2. Subtract \( 1 \) to adjust for the rounding:  
   \[
   14 - 1 = 13
   \]
3. Double-check the calculation:  
   - Adding \( 4 + 10 = 14 \) is correct.  
   - Subtracting \( 1 \) gives \( 13 \), which matches the expected result.

**Potential pitfalls:** This method introduces an extra step, which could lead to errors if the adjustment is forgotten or miscalculated.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct, requires fewer steps, and is less prone to error. It is the most efficient and reliable approach for this problem.  
- **Method 2** is useful for mental math when dealing with larger or more complex numbers, but it is unnecessary here due to the simplicity of the numbers involved.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice.  
- Add the number of games attended this year and last year:  
  \[
  4 + 9 = 13
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using both methods:  
  - Method 1: \( 4 + 9 = 13 \).  
  - Method 2: \( 4 + 10 = 14 \), then \( 14 - 1 = 13 \).  
- Both methods yield the same result.

Q: Alyssa 's dog had puppies . She gave 7 to her friends . She now has 5 puppies . How many puppies did she have to start with ? 
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Alyssa's dog had puppies.
- Alyssa gave 7 puppies to her friends.
- Alyssa now has 5 puppies remaining.
- We are tasked with finding how many puppies Alyssa had to start with.
- Assumptions:
  - No puppies were lost, sold, or otherwise unaccounted for.
  - The total number of puppies Alyssa started with equals the number she gave away plus the number she has now.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Addition Approach**  
- If Alyssa gave away 7 puppies and now has 5, the total number of puppies she started with is the sum of these two quantities: 7 (given away) + 5 (remaining).

**Method 2: Reverse Subtraction Approach**  
- Assume Alyssa started with an unknown number of puppies, denoted as \( x \).  
- Subtract the 7 puppies she gave away from \( x \), and set the result equal to the 5 puppies she has now:  
  \( x - 7 = 5 \).  
- Solve for \( x \) to find the starting number of puppies.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Addition Approach**
1. Alyssa gave away 7 puppies and has 5 left.
2. Add these two quantities:  
   \( 7 + 5 = 12 \).
3. This means Alyssa started with 12 puppies.
4. **Check**: If Alyssa started with 12 puppies and gave away 7, she would have \( 12 - 7 = 5 \) puppies left, which matches the problem statement.

**Potential Pitfalls**: This method is straightforward and has no significant pitfalls as long as the addition is performed correctly.

---

#### **Method 2: Reverse Subtraction Approach**
1. Let \( x \) represent the number of puppies Alyssa started with.
2. Subtract the 7 puppies she gave away:  
   \( x - 7 = 5 \).
3. Solve for \( x \):  
   Add 7 to both sides of the equation:  
   \( x = 5 + 7 \).  
   \( x = 12 \).
4. **Check**: If Alyssa started with 12 puppies and gave away 7, she would have \( 12 - 7 = 5 \) puppies left, which matches the problem statement.

**Potential Pitfalls**: This method requires correctly setting up and solving the equation. Errors could occur if the subtraction or addition is mishandled.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1 (Addition Approach)** is more intuitive and direct. It simply involves summing the given and remaining puppies, which is easy to understand and execute.
- **Method 2 (Reverse Subtraction Approach)** is slightly more abstract, as it requires setting up and solving an equation. While it is equally valid, it may be less intuitive for some people.
- Both methods are reliable and lead to the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient and easier to explain.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

- **Method 1** is the better method due to its simplicity and clarity.
- Solution:  
  \( 7 + 5 = 12 \).  
  Alyssa started with 12 puppies.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- If Alyssa started with 12 puppies and gave away 7, she would have \( 12 - 7 = 5 \) puppies left. This matches the problem statement, confirming the solution is correct.

---

The answer is **12**.

Q: There are 34 dogwood trees currently in the park . Park workers will plant 49 dogwood trees today . How many dogwood trees will the park have when the workers are finished ? 
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The park currently has **34 dogwood trees**.
- Park workers will plant **49 additional dogwood trees** today.
- We assume no trees are removed, die, or are otherwise lost during the process.
- The total number of dogwood trees in the park after planting will be the sum of the current trees and the newly planted trees.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Use direct addition. Add the current number of trees (34) to the number of trees being planted (49): \( 34 + 49 \).

**Method 2:** Break the addition into smaller, more manageable steps. For example:
- Add 30 (from 34) to 49 first, then add the remaining 4.
- Alternatively, round 49 to 50, add 34 to 50, and subtract 1 to adjust for the rounding.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition**
1. Start with the current number of trees: \( 34 \).
2. Add the number of trees being planted: \( 34 + 49 \).
3. Perform the addition:
   - Add the ones place: \( 4 + 9 = 13 \). Write down 3 and carry over 1.
   - Add the tens place: \( 3 + 4 = 7 \), then add the carried-over 1: \( 7 + 1 = 8 \).
   - The result is \( 83 \).
4. Double-check the calculation:
   - Re-add \( 34 + 49 \) using the same process: \( 4 + 9 = 13 \), carry 1; \( 3 + 4 = 7 \), add 1 to get \( 83 \).
   - The calculation checks out.

#### **Method 2: Break the addition into smaller steps**
1. Break \( 34 + 49 \) into smaller parts:
   - Add \( 30 \) (from \( 34 \)) to \( 49 \): \( 30 + 49 = 79 \).
   - Add the remaining \( 4 \): \( 79 + 4 = 83 \).
2. Alternatively, round \( 49 \) to \( 50 \):
   - Add \( 34 + 50 = 84 \).
   - Subtract \( 1 \) to adjust for rounding: \( 84 - 1 = 83 \).
3. Double-check the calculation:
   - Using the first breakdown: \( 30 + 49 = 79 \), \( 79 + 4 = 83 \).
   - Using the rounding method: \( 34 + 50 = 84 \), \( 84 - 1 = 83 \).
   - Both approaches confirm the result.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Direct addition):** This method is straightforward and efficient, especially when working with smaller numbers like \( 34 \) and \( 49 \). It minimizes intermediate steps and is less prone to errors.
- **Method 2 (Breaking into smaller steps):** This method is useful for mental math or when working with larger numbers. However, it introduces more intermediate steps, which can increase the chance of errors if not carefully managed.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for this problem because it is simpler and more direct.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1 (Direct addition):**
1. Add \( 34 + 49 \):
   - \( 4 + 9 = 13 \), carry 1.
   - \( 3 + 4 = 7 \), add the carried-over 1: \( 7 + 1 = 8 \).
   - The result is \( 83 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Re-add \( 34 + 49 \) using the same process: \( 4 + 9 = 13 \), carry 1; \( 3 + 4 = 7 \), add 1 to get \( 83 \).
- Verify using Method 2:
   - \( 30 + 49 = 79 \), \( 79 + 4 = 83 \).
   - \( 34 + 50 = 84 \), \( 84 - 1 = 83 \).
- The calculations are consistent, and the result is reasonable given the problem.

---

The park will have **83 dogwood trees** when the workers are finished.

Q: A restaurant served 7 slices of pie during lunch and 5 during dinner today . It served 8 of them yesterday . How many slices of pie were served today ? 
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The restaurant served **7 slices of pie during lunch** today.
- The restaurant served **5 slices of pie during dinner** today.
- The restaurant served **8 slices of pie yesterday** (this is not relevant to today's total but is included for context).
- We are tasked with finding the **total number of slices served today**.
- Assumption: No slices were served outside of lunch and dinner today.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Add the number of slices served during lunch and dinner directly:  
\[ \text{Total slices today} = \text{Lunch slices} + \text{Dinner slices} \]

**Method 2:**  
Estimate the total by rounding the numbers to simpler values, then adjust for the difference:  
- Approximate 7 slices (lunch) as 10 and 5 slices (dinner) as 5.  
- Add the approximations, then subtract the overestimation.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition**
1. Add the slices served during lunch and dinner:  
   \[
   7 + 5 = 12
   \]
2. Double-check the calculation:  
   - Start with 7, then count up by 5: \( 7 + 1 = 8, 8 + 1 = 9, 9 + 1 = 10, 10 + 1 = 11, 11 + 1 = 12 \).  
   - The result is confirmed as **12**.

**Potential pitfalls:**  
- This method is straightforward, but errors could occur if the addition is rushed or miscalculated.

---

#### **Method 2: Estimation and adjustment**
1. Approximate the slices served during lunch and dinner:  
   - Round 7 (lunch) to 10.  
   - Keep 5 (dinner) as is.  
   - Add the approximations:  
     \[
     10 + 5 = 15
     \]
2. Adjust for the overestimation:  
   - The lunch estimate (10) is 3 higher than the actual value (7).  
   - Subtract the overestimation:  
     \[
     15 - 3 = 12
     \]
3. Double-check the calculation:  
   - The adjusted total matches the direct addition result of **12**.

**Potential pitfalls:**  
- Estimation introduces an extra step, which could lead to errors if the adjustment is not applied correctly.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves straightforward addition without intermediate steps.  
- **Method 2** is useful for mental math but introduces rounding and adjustment, which can complicate the process unnecessarily.  
- For this problem, **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and reliability.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:  
\[
7 + 5 = 12
\]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate: \( 7 + 5 = 12 \).  
- Verify the logic: The problem only asks for today's total, so yesterday's data is irrelevant.  
- The result is reasonable and consistent across both methods.

---

A_model:

  - The loss of balloons only affects the orange balloons, not the blue ones.
  - No additional balloons are gained or lost during this process.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

- Subtract the number of orange balloons lost (2) from the initial number of orange balloons (9).
- This method is straightforward and involves a single subtraction operation.

**Method 2: Step-by-step breakdown.**

- Then, subtract this from the initial total of orange balloons (9) in a step-by-step manner, breaking the subtraction into smaller parts for clarity.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

2. Subtract the number of orange balloons lost: **9 - 2**.

   - Subtract 1 balloon: **9 - 1 = 8**.
   - Subtract another balloon: **8 - 1 = 7**.

Q: Tori is a school janitor . Last week , she picked up a total of 1576 pieces of trash . If she picked up 344 pieces of trash in the classrooms , how many pieces of trash did Tori pick up outside the classrooms ? 
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Tori picked up a total of **1576 pieces of trash** last week.
- She picked up **344 pieces of trash in the classrooms**.
- We are tasked with finding how many pieces of trash Tori picked up **outside the classrooms**.
- Assumptions:
  - All trash picked up is accounted for in the total of 1576.
  - No trash is double-counted or unaccounted for.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Subtraction Method**  
- Subtract the number of pieces of trash picked up in the classrooms (344) from the total number of pieces of trash (1576).  
- This method directly calculates the trash picked up outside the classrooms.

**Method 2: Complementary Addition Method**  
- Start with the number of pieces of trash picked up in the classrooms (344).  
- Add an unknown number (x) to reach the total of 1576. Solve for x using the equation: \( 344 + x = 1576 \).  
- This method uses algebra to find the missing value.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction Method**
1. Start with the total number of pieces of trash: **1576**.
2. Subtract the number of pieces picked up in the classrooms:  
   \[
   1576 - 344 = 1232
   \]
3. Double-check the subtraction:  
   - Break it into smaller steps:  
     \[
     1576 - 344 = (1576 - 300) - 44 = 1276 - 44 = 1232
     \]
4. Verify the result by adding the classroom trash back to the outside trash:  
   \[
   1232 + 344 = 1576
   \]
   The calculation checks out.

#### **Method 2: Complementary Addition Method**
1. Set up the equation:  
   \[
   344 + x = 1576
   \]
2. Solve for \( x \) by isolating it:  
   \[
   x = 1576 - 344
   \]
3. Perform the subtraction:  
   \[
   x = 1232
   \]
4. Double-check by substituting \( x = 1232 \) back into the equation:  
   \[
   344 + 1232 = 1576
   \]
   The calculation checks out.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Subtraction Method)** is more direct and efficient, as it involves a single subtraction operation. It is less prone to error and requires fewer steps.
- **Method 2 (Complementary Addition Method)** is slightly more complex because it introduces an equation and requires solving for a variable. While it works, it is less efficient for this straightforward problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and clarity.
- Using Method 1:  
   \[
   1576 - 344 = 1232
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Add the classroom trash (344) to the outside trash (1232) to confirm the total:  
   \[
   344 + 1232 = 1576
   \]
- The result is consistent with the given total, so the solution is correct.

---

The answer is **1232**.

     \]

     \[
     224 + 0.87 = 224.87
     \]
   - The result checks out.

A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Melanie initially had **19 dimes** in her bank.
- Her dad gave her **39 dimes**.
- Her mother gave her **25 dimes**.
- We assume no dimes were lost, spent, or removed during this process.
- The goal is to find the total number of dimes Melanie has now.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Add the initial number of dimes to the dimes given by her dad and then add the dimes given by her mother:  
\[ \text{Total dimes} = 19 + 39 + 25 \]

**Method 2:**  
Group the dimes given by her dad and mother first, then add the result to the initial number of dimes:  
\[ \text{Total dimes} = (39 + 25) + 19 \]

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition of all values**
1. Start with the initial number of dimes: **19**.
2. Add the dimes given by her dad:  
   \[ 19 + 39 = 58 \]
3. Add the dimes given by her mother:  
   \[ 58 + 25 = 83 \]
4. Double-check the calculations:  
   - Adding 19 and 39:  
     \[ 19 + 39 = 58 \]  
   - Adding 58 and 25:  
     \[ 58 + 25 = 83 \]  
   The calculations are consistent.

#### **Method 2: Grouping dad's and mom's contributions first**
1. Add the dimes given by her dad and mother:  
   \[ 39 + 25 = 64 \]
2. Add this result to the initial number of dimes:  
   \[ 64 + 19 = 83 \]
3. Double-check the calculations:  
   - Adding 39 and 25:  
     \[ 39 + 25 = 64 \]  
   - Adding 64 and 19:  
     \[ 64 + 19 = 83 \]  
   The calculations are consistent.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and involves adding the numbers in the order they are presented. It is simple and intuitive but requires keeping track of intermediate results.
- **Method 2** groups the contributions from her dad and mother first, which can simplify mental math by reducing the number of steps. It is particularly useful when working with larger numbers.

Both methods are equally reliable and efficient for this problem. The choice depends on personal preference, but **Method 2** may be slightly easier for mental calculations.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
We will use **Method 2** for its simplicity in grouping contributions.

1. Add the dimes given by her dad and mother:  
   \[ 39 + 25 = 64 \]
2. Add this result to the initial number of dimes:  
   \[ 64 + 19 = 83 \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using **Method 1**:  
  \[ 19 + 39 = 58 \]  
  \[ 58 + 25 = 83 \]  
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution is correct.

Q: Vince 's bus ride to school is 0.625 mile and Zachary 's bus ride is 0.5 mile . How much longer is Vince 's bus ride than Zachary 's ? 
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Vince's bus ride to school is **0.625 miles**.
- Zachary's bus ride to school is **0.5 miles**.
- We are tasked with finding how much longer Vince's bus ride is compared to Zachary's.
- Assumptions:
  - Both distances are measured in miles.
  - The difference is calculated as Vince's distance minus Zachary's distance.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct subtraction**  
- Subtract Zachary's distance from Vince's distance: \( 0.625 - 0.5 \).  
- This method directly calculates the difference between the two distances.

**Method 2: Convert to fractions and subtract**  
- Convert the decimal values \( 0.625 \) and \( 0.5 \) into fractions: \( 0.625 = \frac{625}{1000} = \frac{5}{8} \) and \( 0.5 = \frac{1}{2} \).  
- Subtract the fractions: \( \frac{5}{8} - \frac{1}{2} \).  
- This method uses fraction arithmetic to find the difference.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Write the subtraction: \( 0.625 - 0.5 \).
2. Align the decimal points and subtract:
   \[
   0.625 - 0.500 = 0.125
   \]
3. Result: The difference is \( 0.125 \) miles.
4. Check: Add the difference back to Zachary's distance to confirm:
   \[
   0.5 + 0.125 = 0.625
   \]
   This confirms the calculation is correct.

#### **Method 2: Convert to fractions and subtract**
1. Convert \( 0.625 \) to a fraction:
   \[
   0.625 = \frac{625}{1000} = \frac{5}{8}
   \]
2. Convert \( 0.5 \) to a fraction:
   \[
   0.5 = \frac{1}{2}
   \]
3. Find a common denominator for \( \frac{5}{8} \) and \( \frac{1}{2} \). The least common denominator (LCD) is 8.
   - Rewrite \( \frac{1}{2} \) as \( \frac{4}{8} \).
4. Subtract the fractions:
   \[
   \frac{5}{8} - \frac{4}{8} = \frac{1}{8}
   \]
5. Convert \( \frac{1}{8} \) back to a decimal:
   \[
   \frac{1}{8} = 0.125
   \]
6. Result: The difference is \( 0.125 \) miles.
7. Check: Add the difference back to Zachary's distance to confirm:
   \[
   0.5 + 0.125 = 0.625
   \]
   This confirms the calculation is correct.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Direct subtraction)** is simpler and faster because it works directly with the decimal values. It avoids the extra steps of converting to fractions and finding a common denominator.
- **Method 2 (Fraction subtraction)** is more complex but useful if working in a context where fractions are required. However, it introduces more steps and potential for error during conversion or arithmetic.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and efficiency.
- Using Method 1:
  \[
  0.625 - 0.5 = 0.125
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Add the difference back to Zachary's distance:
  \[
  0.5 + 0.125 = 0.625
  \]
  This confirms the result is correct and reasonable.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **0.125 miles**.

  - The $12.32 for peaches is the final amount after applying the coupon.  
  - There are no additional discounts, taxes, or fees.

     \[
     26 + 0.86 = 26.86
     \]
4. Result: Sally spent **$26.86** in total if we include the original peach price.

Q: Last week Tom had 74 dollars . He washed cars over the weekend and now has 86 dollars . How much money did he make washing cars ? 
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Tom started with **74 dollars** last week.
- After washing cars over the weekend, he now has **86 dollars**.
- The amount of money Tom made washing cars is the difference between his current total and his starting amount.
- We assume no other sources of income or expenses occurred during this time.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Subtraction  
- Subtract the starting amount (74 dollars) from the current total (86 dollars) to find the amount Tom earned.

**Method 2:** Reverse addition  
- Start with the initial amount (74 dollars) and add increments until reaching the current total (86 dollars). The sum of the increments represents the money Tom made.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction**
1. Subtract the starting amount from the current total:  
   \( 86 - 74 = 12 \).
2. Double-check the subtraction:  
   - Break it into smaller steps:  
     \( 86 - 70 = 16 \), then \( 16 - 4 = 12 \).  
   - Verify by adding the result back to the starting amount:  
     \( 74 + 12 = 86 \).  
3. The result is consistent, so Tom made **12 dollars**.

#### **Method 2: Reverse addition**
1. Start with the initial amount (74 dollars) and add increments to reach the current total (86 dollars):  
   - \( 74 + 10 = 84 \).  
   - \( 84 + 2 = 86 \).  
2. Add the increments together:  
   \( 10 + 2 = 12 \).  
3. Double-check by reversing the process:  
   - Subtract the total increments (12) from the current total (86):  
     \( 86 - 12 = 74 \).  
4. The result is consistent, so Tom made **12 dollars**.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Subtraction)** is more direct and efficient, requiring fewer steps and less room for error. It is the most straightforward way to calculate the difference between two numbers.
- **Method 2 (Reverse addition)** is useful for mental math or when working without paper, but it involves more steps and is less efficient for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and reliability.
- Subtract the starting amount from the current total:  
  \( 86 - 74 = 12 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify by adding the result (12 dollars) back to the starting amount:  
  \( 74 + 12 = 86 \).  
- The calculation checks out, and the result is reasonable given the context.

---

The answer is **12**.

**Method 2:** Break the numbers into smaller, more manageable parts (e.g., hundreds, tens, and ones), add them separately, and then combine the results.

1. Add the two numbers directly:  
   \( 123 + 104 \).  
2. Perform the addition step by step:  
   - Add the ones place: \( 3 + 4 = 7 \).  
   - Add the tens place: \( 2 + 0 = 2 \).

     ```
       123
     + 104
     -----

     ```

   - Add the tens: \( 20 + 0 = 20 \).  
   - Add the ones: \( 3 + 4 = 7 \).

   - \( 200 + 20 + 7 = 227 \).

     \( 200 + 20 = 220 \), \( 220 + 7 = 227 \).

- Using **Method 1**:  
  \( 123 + 104 = 227 \).

  - \( 123 + 104 = 227 \) using column addition.  
  - The result is consistent across all methods.

- It rained 0.4 inches on Sunday.  
- We are tasked with finding the total rainfall for Saturday and Sunday combined.  
- Assume the measurements are accurate and there is no overlap or additional rainfall beyond what is stated.

---

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
**Method 1:** Direct addition of the rainfall amounts: Add 0.2 inches and 0.4 inches together.  
**Method 2:** Convert the decimal values into fractions (e.g., 0.2 = 1/5 and 0.4 = 2/5), add the fractions, and convert the result back to a decimal.

---

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
- Add the two decimal values directly:  
  \( 0.2 + 0.4 = 0.6 \).  
- Double-check the addition:  
  - Start with 0.2 and add 0.4 incrementally:  
    \( 0.2 + 0.1 = 0.3 \), \( 0.3 + 0.1 = 0.4 \), \( 0.4 + 0.2 = 0.6 \).  
- Potential pitfalls: Ensure proper alignment of decimal places during addition.  

**Method 2:**  
- Convert the decimals to fractions:  
  \( 0.2 = \frac{1}{5} \), \( 0.4 = \frac{2}{5} \).  
- Add the fractions:  
  \( \frac{1}{5} + \frac{2}{5} = \frac{3}{5} \).  
- Convert the result back to a decimal:  
  \( \frac{3}{5} = 0.6 \).  
- Double-check the fraction addition:  
  - The denominators are the same, so add the numerators: \( 1 + 2 = 3 \).  
  - The result is \( \frac{3}{5} \), which equals 0.6 when converted to a decimal.  
- Potential pitfalls: Ensure correct fraction conversion and addition.

---

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and efficient, as it directly adds the decimal values without requiring conversion.  
- **Method 2** is more complex due to the need for fraction conversion and reconversion to decimals, which introduces additional steps and potential errors.  
- For this problem, **Method 1** is better because it is simpler and avoids unnecessary complexity.

---

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- Using **Method 1**, add the rainfall amounts directly:  
  \( 0.2 + 0.4 = 0.6 \).  

---

Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.  
- Re-add the values incrementally: \( 0.2 + 0.1 = 0.3 \), \( 0.3 + 0.1 = 0.4 \), \( 0.4 + 0.2 = 0.6 \).  
- Verify the result using Method 2 (fraction addition): \( \frac{1}{5} + \frac{2}{5} = \frac{3}{5} = 0.6 \).  
- The result is consistent across both methods and reasonable given the context.

Q: There are 27 pencils in the drawer . Nancy placed 45 pencils in the drawer . How many pencils are now there in total ? 
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- There are initially **27 pencils** in the drawer.
- Nancy places an additional **45 pencils** in the drawer.
- We assume no pencils are removed, lost, or broken during this process.
- The goal is to find the **total number of pencils** in the drawer after Nancy adds her pencils.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Use direct addition. Add the initial number of pencils (27) to the number of pencils Nancy added (45). This is the most straightforward approach.

**Method 2:** Break the addition into smaller, easier steps. For example, decompose 45 into 40 and 5, then add incrementally: 27 + 40 = 67, and 67 + 5 = 72. This method is useful for mental math or when working without a calculator.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct Addition**
1. Start with the initial number of pencils: **27**.
2. Add the number of pencils Nancy placed in the drawer: **27 + 45**.
3. Perform the addition:
   - Add the ones place: \( 7 + 5 = 12 \) (write down 2, carry over 1).
   - Add the tens place: \( 2 + 4 = 6 \), then add the carried-over 1: \( 6 + 1 = 7 \).
   - The result is **72**.
4. Double-check the calculation:
   - Re-add: \( 27 + 45 = 72 \). The result is consistent.

#### **Method 2: Incremental Addition**
1. Decompose 45 into 40 and 5.
2. Add the first part (40) to the initial number of pencils:
   - \( 27 + 40 = 67 \).
3. Add the second part (5) to the result:
   - \( 67 + 5 = 72 \).
4. Double-check the calculation:
   - Re-add incrementally: \( 27 + 40 = 67 \), \( 67 + 5 = 72 \). The result is consistent.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves a single calculation. It is ideal when using a calculator or when comfortable with basic addition.
- **Method 2** is more intuitive for mental math, as breaking the problem into smaller steps reduces the chance of error. However, it requires more intermediate steps and is slightly less efficient.

For this problem, **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and speed.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:
1. Add the initial number of pencils (27) to the number Nancy added (45):  
   \( 27 + 45 = 72 \).
2. Double-check the calculation:
   - Add the ones place: \( 7 + 5 = 12 \) (write down 2, carry over 1).
   - Add the tens place: \( 2 + 4 = 6 \), then add the carried-over 1: \( 6 + 1 = 7 \).
   - The result is **72**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify using **Method 2**:  
  \( 27 + 40 = 67 \), \( 67 + 5 = 72 \). The result matches.
- The result is reasonable, as adding 45 pencils to 27 pencils should yield a larger total, and 72 is consistent with expectations.

The answer is **72**.

Q: Pamela bought 9.8 ounces of sugar , and she spilled 5.2 ounces of it on the floor . How much is ? 
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Pamela initially bought **9.8 ounces** of sugar.
- She spilled **5.2 ounces** of sugar on the floor.
- We are tasked with finding how much sugar Pamela has left.
- Assumptions:
  - All spilled sugar is lost and cannot be recovered.
  - No additional sugar was added or removed beyond the spilled amount.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Subtraction  
- Subtract the amount of sugar spilled (5.2 ounces) from the total amount Pamela initially had (9.8 ounces). This directly gives the remaining sugar.

**Method 2:** Complementary addition  
- Think of the problem as: "What amount, when added to 5.2 ounces, equals 9.8 ounces?" Solve for the missing amount using addition.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction**
1. Start with the total amount of sugar: **9.8 ounces**.
2. Subtract the spilled amount:  
   \[
   9.8 - 5.2
   \]
3. Perform the subtraction step by step:
   - Subtract the tenths place: \( 8 - 2 = 6 \).
   - Subtract the ones place: \( 9 - 5 = 4 \).
   - Combine the results: \( 4.6 \).
4. Result: Pamela has **4.6 ounces** of sugar left.

**Check for errors:**  
- Re-add the spilled amount to the remaining sugar:  
  \[
  4.6 + 5.2 = 9.8
  \]
- The calculation checks out.

---

#### **Method 2: Complementary addition**
1. Think of the problem as:  
   \[
   5.2 + x = 9.8
   \]
   Solve for \( x \), the remaining sugar.
2. Rearrange the equation:  
   \[
   x = 9.8 - 5.2
   \]
3. Perform the subtraction (same as Method 1):  
   \[
   x = 4.6
   \]
4. Result: Pamela has **4.6 ounces** of sugar left.

**Check for errors:**  
- Add the spilled amount to the remaining sugar:  
  \[
  4.6 + 5.2 = 9.8
  \]
- The calculation checks out.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Subtraction):**  
  - Direct and straightforward.
  - Involves fewer steps and is less prone to error.
  - Best suited for problems involving "how much is left" after a loss.

- **Method 2 (Complementary addition):**  
  - Useful for conceptualizing the problem differently, especially when subtraction is less intuitive.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.
